@@51729601 <h> You are here <h> Free trade is war <p> On Monday , seven antiprivatization activists were arrested in Soweto for blocking the installation of prepaid water meters . The meters are a privatized answer to the fact that millions of poor South Africans can not pay their water bills . <p> The new gadgets work like pay-as-you-go cell phones , only instead of having a dead phone when you run out of money , you have dead people , sickened by drinking cholera-infested water . <p> On the same day South Africa 's ? water warriors ? were locked up , Argentina 's negotiations with the International Monetary Fund bogged down . The sticking point was rate hikes for privatized utility companies . In a country where 50 per cent of the population is living in poverty , the IMF is demanding that multinational water and electricity companies be allowed to increase their rates by a staggering 30 per cent . <p> At trade summits , debates about privatization can seem wonkish and abstract . On the ground , they are as clear and urgent as the right to survive . <p> After September 11 , right-wing pundits @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ were gleefully informed that in times of war , no one would care about frivolous issues like water privatization . Much of the U.S. antiwar movement fell into a related trap : Now was not the time to focus on divisive economic debates , it was time to come together to call for peace . <p> All this nonsense ends in Canc ? ? n this week , when thousands of activists converge to declare that the brutal economic model advanced by the World Trade Organization is itself a form of war . <p> War because privatization and deregulation kill ? by pushing up prices on necessities like water and medicines and pushing down prices on raw commodities like coffee , making small farms unsustainable . War because those who resist and ? refuse to disappear , ? as the Zapatistas say , are routinely arrested , beaten and even killed . War because when this kind of low-intensity repression fails to clear the path to corporate liberation , the real wars begin . <p> The global antiwar protests that surprised the world on February 15 grew out of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to the World Social Forum . And despite attempts to keep the movements separate , their only future lies in the convergence represented by Canc ? ? n . Past movements have tried to fight wars without confronting the economic interests behind them , or to win economic justice without confronting military power . Today 's activists , already experts at following the money , are n't making the same mistake . <p> Take Rachel Corrie . Although she is engraved in our minds as the 23-year-old in an orange jacket with the courage to face down Israeli bulldozers , Corrie had already glimpsed a larger threat looming behind the military hardware . ? I think it is counterproductive to only draw attention to crisis points ? the demolition of houses , shootings , overt violence , ? she wrote in one of her last e-mails . ? So much of what happens in Rafah is related to this slow elimination of people 's ability to survive .... Water , in particular , seems critical and invisible. ? The 1999 Battle of Seattle was Corrie 's first big protest . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ herself not only to see the repression on the surface but to dig deeper , to search for the economic interests served by the Israeli attacks . This digging ? interrupted by her murder ? led Corrie to the wells in nearby settlements , which she suspected of diverting precious water from Gaza to Israeli agricultural land . <p> Similarly , when Washington started handing out reconstruction contracts in Iraq , veterans of the globalization debate spotted the underlying agenda in the familiar names of deregulation and privatization pushers Bechtel and Halliburton . If these guys are leading the charge , it means Iraq is being sold off , not rebuilt . Even those who opposed the war exclusively for how it was waged ( without UN approval , with insufficient evidence that Iraq posed an imminent threat ) now can not help but see why it was waged : to implement the very same policies being protested in Canc ? ? n ? mass privatization , unrestricted access for multinationals and drastic public-sector cutbacks . As Robert Fisk recently wrote in The Independent , Paul Bremer 's uniform says @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? <p> Occupied Iraq is being turned into a twisted laboratory for freebase free-market economics , much as Chile was for Milton Friedman 's ? Chicago boys ? after the 1973 coup . Friedman called it ? shock treatment , ? though , as in Iraq , it was actually armed robbery of the shellshocked . <p> Speaking of Chile , the Bush Administration has let it be known that if the Canc ? ? n meetings fail , it will simply barrel ahead with more bilateral free-trade deals , like the one just signed with Chile . Insignificant in economic terms , the deal 's real power is as a wedge : Already , Washington is using it to bully Brazil and Argentina into supporting the Free Trade Area of the Americas or risk being left behind . <p> Thirty years have passed since that other September 11 , when Gen. Augusto Pinochet , with the help of the CIA , brought the free market to Chile ? with blood and fire , ? as they say in Latin America . That terror is paying dividends to this day @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ most pliant country in the region , willing to do Washington 's bidding even as its neighbors reject neoliberalism at the ballot box and on the streets . <p> In August 1976 , an article appeared in this magazine written by Orlando Letelier , former foreign affairs minister in Salvador Allende 's overthrown government . Letelier was frustrated with an international community that professed horror at Pinochet 's human rights abuses but supported his free-market policies , refusing to see ? the brutal force required to achieve these goals . Repression for the majorities and ' economic freedom ' for small privileged groups are in Chile two sides of the same coin. ? Less than a month later , Letelier was killed by a car bomb in Washington , DC . <p> The greatest enemies of terror never lose sight of the economic interests served by violence , or the violence of capitalism itself . Letelier understood that . So did Rachel Corrie . As our movements converge in Canc ? ? n , so must we . <p> Thank you for reading this story ... <p> More people are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we have never put up a paywall -- at rabble we 've always believed in making our reporting and analysis free to all , while striving to make it sustainable as well . Media is n't free to produce . rabble 's total budget is likely less than what big corporate media spend on photocopying ( we kid you not ! ) and we do not have any major foundation , sponsor or angel investor . Our main supporters are people and organizations -- like you . This is why we need your help . You are what keep us sustainable . <p> rabble.ca has staked its existence on you . We live or die on community support -- your support ! We get hundreds of thousands of visitors and we believe in them . We believe in you . We believe people will put in what they can for the greater good . We call that sustainable . <p> So what is the easy answer for us ? Depend on a community of visitors who care passionately about media that amplifies the voices of people struggling for change and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people who visit rabble care enough to contribute a bit then it works for everyone . <p> And so we 're asking you if you could make a donation , right now , to help us carry forward on our mission . Make a donation today .

The victims included 55 citizens of this country and 30 permanent residents , among 138 passengers heading to Canada via Kyiv , many of them students returning after the Christmas break . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Iranian authorities originally claimed the plane&amp;rsquo ; s demise was an accident , but under pressure admitted that air-defence forces had mistakenly fired two missiles at the Boeing 737 amid tension with the United States . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Nearly six months later , Iran has yet to turn over the two black boxes from the doomed plane to a country that has the technology to extract their data , despite having promised to do so . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canadian Transportation Minister Marc Garneau urged Iran last week to transfer the plane&amp;rsquo ; s data recorders , citing its obligations under ICAO rules . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 640 " height= " 480 " src= " https : **33;3103;TOOLONG ... frameborder= " 0 " allow= " accelerometer ; autoplay ; encrypted-media ; gyroscope ; picture-in-picture " **34;3138;TOOLONG &lt;p&gt; Esmaeilion said he is &amp;ldquo ; 100 per cent certain&amp;rdquo ; that officials took belongings of passengers . He cited early photographs of the crash scene that showed numerous possessions scattered about , and news video of an official searching through hand luggage . Some families saw photos of loved ones&amp;rsquo ; passports but never got them back , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One official involved in sorting through the wreckage found the carry-on bag of a victim he knew , containing thousands of dollars in cash . He offered to return it to the family , but superiors said they would keep the material , said Esmaeilion . The relatives eventually received only an empty bag . Photos of remains released soon after the crash showed victims wearing wedding rings and other jewelry , which were never returned , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This is a story that we have heard from lots of family members . " data-portal-copyright= " Peter Thompson " **28;3463;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78541559 " /&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : //www.youtu ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; The federal government has taken new steps to make it easier for international students and other temporary foreign residents to receive emergency benefits , another sign of Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s determination to disburse the payments quickly and widely . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Such short-term immigrants need only give their word they have a valid work permit or have applied for a renewed one to obtain the Canada Emergency Response Benefit ( CERB ) , says a memo sent this week to staff vetting the claims . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Until last Thursday , they had to email Employment and Social Development Canada an image of their valid work or work/study permit , or confirmation they had applied to renew an expired one . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But a memo sent to Employment and Social Development Canada officials handling CERB applications said that condition is waived &amp;ldquo ; effective immediately&amp;rdquo ; and agents &amp;ldquo ; are only required to verbally obtain work permit details . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The directive applies to everyone who claims to meet the programs other requirements and has a &amp;ldquo ; 900-series&amp;rdquo ; social insurance number &amp;mdash ; people ranging from students to refugee claimants to temporary foreign workers and executives transferred from other countries . None are Canadian citizens or permanent residents . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One source familiar with the system said people with valid permits would typically email proof within a few minutes , barely slowing the process . But now there is no way for staff to verify whether someone is in the country legally , the person said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And if an applicant does receive the $2,000-a-month payments inappropriately and then leaves Canada , it would be virtually impossible to recover the money , said the source , who&amp;rsquo ; s not authorized to discuss internal matters and asked not to be named . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There is a big , big opening for fraud left , right and centre . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Outside experts offered differing opinions , with one immigrant advocate calling it an &amp;ldquo ; excellent&amp;rdquo ; policy that should get important help to temporary residents faster , and an immigration lawyer saying it shows an &amp;ldquo ; astonishing&amp;rdquo ; disregard for taxpayer funds . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Maya Dura , a spokeswoman for Ahmed Hussen , the families , children and social devlopment minister , said such claimants &amp;ldquo ; may be asked to provide additional documentation to verify their eligibility at a future date . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The Government of Canada will , whether it be in the upcoming weeks or at tax time next year , reconcile accounts and make sure people did not defraud the CERB , &amp;rdquo ; she added via email . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Asked about 900-series residents generally , Dura provided statistics just for international students , saying 39,319 had applied for CERB through ESDC by May 18 , and 30,645 have received payments so far . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The CERB program has wide support from all parties as a way to soften the blow for people left jobless or unable to find work by the pandemic and lockdowns . It provides $500 a week to people who &amp;ldquo ; have stopped working&amp;rdquo ; because of COVID-19 , so long as they made $5,000 within the previous 12 months and did not quit voluntarily . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But there has been increasing scrutiny of the program in recent days amid revelations about how it&amp;rsquo ; s being managed . Previous memos , obtained by the &lt;em&gt;National Post&lt;/em&gt; , directed staff to approve applicants even if they see evidence of potential abuse , and even if people quit their jobs voluntarily or were fired for alleged misconduct , seemingly contrary to CERB rules . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Prime Minister Justin Trudeau has said the government will claw back unwarranted or fraudulent payments later , but had to get cheques out quickly because of the millions of people put out of work . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sergio Karas , a Toronto immigration lawyer , condemned the latest change , saying it means even an individual who is facing a deportation order or who had already left the country could now obtain CERB. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s truly astonishing , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; The person could potentially be overseas if the payment is going to a Canadian bank account . That is extremely troubling . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; That money is not free , &amp;rdquo ; he added . &amp;ldquo ; That money is going to have to come out of someone&amp;rsquo ; s pocket at some point . It is going to be the taxpayers of Canada , citizens or not . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Debbie Douglas , executive director of the Ontario Council of Agencies Serving Immigrants , praised the policy as &amp;ldquo ; an excellent move by the federal government . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Many of these individuals through no fault of their own are unable to have their SINs and-or work permits renewed during this health emergency , &amp;rdquo ; she said by email . &amp;ldquo ; At the same time many have lost their jobs or have experienced significant reduction in hours of work . Many were or are vulnerable to evictions. &amp;ldquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;3493;TOOLONG ... not impose a stop pay ' : Federal workers ordered to ignore cheating in CERB and EI **27;3528;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;3557;TOOLONG ... Thursday&amp;rsquo ; s directive is &amp;ldquo ; very useful&amp;rdquo ; as it will help speed up CERB cheques for people who haven&amp;rsquo ; t had time to apply for a permit renewal , said Douglas . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The memo last week notes that to be eligible for benefits like Employment Insurance or CERB a temporary resident with a 900-series SIN &amp;ldquo ; must prove they are legally allowed to work in Canada . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But &amp;ldquo ; due to COVID-19 the 900-series SIN procedures have been simplified , &amp;rdquo ; it said in explaining the change to requiring merely verbal proof , &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The government had paid out $39 billion under CERB to more than eight million claimants by May 21. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;em&gt;* Email : &lt;a href= " **34;3684;TOOLONG " **41;3720;TOOLONG Twitter : &lt;a href= " https : **28;3763;TOOLONG " class= " twitter-follow-button " &gt;Tomblackwell ... &lt;/p&gt; Tom BlackwellMon , 25 May 2020 11:00:34 **45;3793;TOOLONG ... and **40;3840;TOOLONG infected , several dead : How COVID-19 has affected the unsung essential workers in retailhttp : **34;3882;TOOLONG ... src= " https : **33;3918;TOOLONG ... title= " Shoppers practice social distancing outside a No Frills location on Wilson Avenue in Toronto during the ongoing Covid 19 pandemic , Wednesday April 1 , 2020. " alt= " Shoppers practice social distancing outside a No Frills location on Wilson Avenue in Toronto during the ongoing Covid 19 pandemic , Wednesday April 1 , 2020. " data-portal-copyright= " Peter J Thompson " **28;3953;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78540349 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; As an associate manager at a No Frills supermarket , Helen Stathopoulos rarely had to confront existential worries on the job . Then came the COVID-19 pandemic. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For two months , she and colleagues in north-end Toronto have faced daily risk of infection in one of the few environments where a locked-down populace routinely congregated in large numbers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; When you go to work , you don&amp;rsquo ; t know what you&amp;rsquo ; re going to come up against , &amp;rdquo ; said Sathopoulos , 41. &amp;ldquo ; It would be a lie to say it doesn&amp;rsquo ; t weigh on us . It does . You think about it all the time &amp;hellip ; Absolutely the fear is there . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Indeed , the legions of grocery and pharmacy workers like her across Canada have been the unsung frontline workers of the pandemic , and their quotidian exposure to the public has had an impact . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As the epidemic slows and other retailers begin to open , the anxiety has lessened . Meanwhile , health-care workers have definitely been the hardest hit by the coronavirus , with thousands sickened. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;3983;TOOLONG ... title= " Helen Stathopoulos , an associate manager at a Toronto No Frills supermarket , says going to work in busy stores throughout the pandemic has often been stressful . Companies say about 500 grocery workers across Canada have caught COVID-19 , while one union says several have died . " alt= " Helen Stathopoulos , an associate manager at a Toronto No Frills supermarket , says going to work in busy stores throughout the pandemic has often been stressful . Companies say about 500 grocery workers across Canada have caught COVID-19 , while one union says several have died . " data-portal-copyright= " Handout " **28;4018;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78540353 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; But based on union estimates and statistics provided by some companies , at least 500 food-retail and pharmacy employees throughout Canada have tested positive and several have died from the disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For many shoppers , as well , doing the groceries has been a stressful activity , with some pointing to such stores as the source of their own infections . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One Toronto man posted &lt;a href= " https : **30;4048;TOOLONG ? ... Facebook &lt;/a&gt; that the only place he could have caught COVID-19 was a supermarket that&amp;rsquo ; s been closed for several days because of an outbreak among staff . He says his immune-compromised wife also contracted the disease , eventually winding up in intensive care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But it remains unclear to what extent supermarkets have contributed to the community spread of COVID-19 , frustrating experts trying to track how the virus disseminates. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The data are not being collected in a systematic way to be able to determine many potential sources of transmission in the community , &amp;rdquo ; said Dr. Jeff Kwong , a family physician and public-health professor at the University of Toronto . &amp;ldquo ; We have the category &amp;lsquo ; Close contact of a confirmed case&amp;rsquo ; but that doesn&amp;rsquo ; t tell us if the contact occurred at home or at work , and the type of work involved . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Union and company representatives argue that protective measures implemented early on in the pandemic &amp;mdash ; from limiting the number of customers in stores to installing Plexiglas shields for cashiers &amp;mdash ; have been generally effective in keeping both employees and customers safe . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In some ways , the pandemic has even been a boon for a workforce that earns relatively little and has seen full-time jobs dwindle , said one union leader . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; More than 80 per cent of grocery employees work part-time , but demand in the now-booming sector has brought full-time hours for many , said Chris MacDonald , assistant to the president of Unifor , which represents about 13,000 supermarket employees . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; All of a sudden , retail workers are making decent money , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; There are things that change the game for retail workers . They all got more hours &amp;hellip ; This COVID-19 has allowed us to highlight the importance of these workers , the necessity of their work . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That said , some have suffered as a result of the pandemic. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Loblaws Inc , which has almost 200,000 employees in 2,500 supermarkets and Shoppers Drug Mart outlets , said 204 of them have tested positive for COVID-19. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Metro Inc. &lt;a href= " https : **33;4080;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;posts details&lt;/a&gt; of infections and the stores or warehouses where they occurred on its website , and as of Wednesday reported 117 COVID-19-postive employees . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sobeys Inc. has a similar &lt;a href= " https : **33;4115;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;online record&lt;/a&gt; &lt;p&gt; Representatives of Costco Canada , Rexall and Pharmasave did not respond to requests for similar information . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Barry Sawyer , a top official with the United Food and Commercial Workers ( UFCW ) union , which represents 160,000 food-retail members , said &amp;ldquo ; less than 10&amp;rdquo ; of them have died from COVID-19. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Many others , though , have paid a psychological price , said Wayne Hanley , president of the UFCW local representing Toronto-area grocery workers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s all about &amp;hellip ; the stress that&amp;rsquo ; s brought on by the unknown when going to work , &amp;nbsp ; whether or not all their colleagues are going to be there , &amp;rdquo ; Still , unions say the companies have worked positively with them to put in place protections , and in many cases boost pay by two dollars an hour . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That willingness to help may have come partly from self-interest , given workers could have chosen to stay home instead and receive the federal government&amp;rsquo ; s Canada Emergency Response Benefit , said MacDonald . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They needed to figure out &amp;hellip ; how do you make it worthwhile for these people who often are making a couple of dollars over minimum wage at best , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; What makes it worthwhile coming to work if you can earn 500 bucks staying at home . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For Stathopoulos , the pandemic has also been a chance to observe people during an historic crisis , from the initial panic that led to hoarding of items like toilet paper to those who selflessly picked up groceries for elderly neighbours . &lt;/p&gt; " **28;5836;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78516603 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; It was 1928 when Scottish scientist Alexander Fleming noticed an odd phenomenon : mould that had formed accidentally in one of his petri dishes was stopping the growth of bacteria . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Fleming had of course just discovered penicillin , and by the mid-1940s the world&amp;rsquo ; s first antibiotic would be in mass production , launching an historic era when most bacterial infections became curable. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As COVID-19 has made painfully clear , viral diseases are a rather different matter . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Partly because of the unique , parasitical qualities of the virus , making drugs to treat infections ranging from the common cold to flu and Ebola has proven a much more daunting task , with a few major successes and many failures . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;5866;TOOLONG ... coronavirus curve flattens , but health officials say worrying trends **27;5901;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;5930;TOOLONG ... Provinces begin to ease restrictions as Canada takes baby steps toward **29;5965;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt; ... An effective medication against COVID-19 could lessen the disease&amp;rsquo ; s death toll and burden on health care in the year or so it until a vaccine comes online . But even the most promising treatment so far , &lt;a href= " https : **33;5996;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;Remdesivir , &lt;/a&gt; may only modestly speed up recovery , trial results released this week suggested . It was invented to treat Ebola and failed at that task . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hopes for ending the pandemic are instead being pinned largely on vaccines , substances that prevent infection rather than treating it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But as the scientific community receives funding to pivot massively toward the novel coronavirus , the anti-viral field in general could take a giant leap forward , a rare silver lining to the pandemic cloud , experts say . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Looking for a way to fight COVID-19 is likely to have spillover benefits for a variety of largely untreatable viruses , they say . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s always a question of dollars for research &amp;mdash ; there&amp;rsquo ; s never enough to do everything you want , &amp;rdquo ; said &lt;a href= " https : **33;6031;TOOLONG ... Duncan&lt;/a&gt; , a Dalhousie University virology professor . &amp;ldquo ; So unfortunate situations like this do focus people&amp;rsquo ; s and politicians&amp;rsquo ; attention on the need to do that &amp;hellip ; I think there will be big consequences . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;6066;TOOLONG ... title= " Two ampules of Ebola drug Remdesivir are pictured during a news conference at the University Hospital Eppendorf ( UKE ) in Hamburg , Germany , April 8 , 2020. " alt= " Two ampules of Ebola drug Remdesivir are pictured during a news conference at the University Hospital Eppendorf ( UKE ) in Hamburg , Germany , April 8 , 2020. " data-portal-copyright= " Ulrich Perrey/Pool via REUTERS " **28;6101;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78516602 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. &lt;a href= " https : **33;6131;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;Gerald Evans&lt;/a&gt; , chair of the infectious-diseases division at Queen&amp;rsquo ; s University medical school , likens the potential explosion in science to what happened when AIDS emerged . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; HIV research really took off in the late &amp;rsquo ; 80s and early &amp;rsquo ; 90s , and taught us how the virus replicates , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; We&amp;rsquo ; re kind of with anti-virals now where antibiotics were in the 1950s &amp;hellip ; This particular ( COVID-19 ) virus is going to really dramatically accelerate a lot of work . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As for existing anti-virals , HIV definitely represents one of the success stories . The infection killed almost everyone who got it 30 years ago , but patients can now live a long and healthy life thanks to the drug regimen most now take . The medicine doesn&amp;rsquo ; t cure the virus , but suppresses it enough that the deadly symptoms of AIDS are kept at bay . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A new generation of Hepatitis C drugs , which come with massive price tags , are another breakthrough , actually curing that disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But what about &amp;ldquo ; acute&amp;rdquo ; viral infections &amp;mdash ; flu , measles or West Nile &amp;mdash ; where there is a short , sharp onset of symptoms and relatively quick recovery , or sometimes death ? &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We really don&amp;rsquo ; t have good anti-virals for any acute viral infections , &amp;rdquo ; said Dr. Srinivas Murthy , who&amp;rsquo ; s heading a Canadian trial of possible COVID-19 drug treatments . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is , said Evans , &amp;ldquo ; a heck of a lot harder&amp;rdquo ; to create an anti-viral than an antibiotic . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Much of the challenge lies in the nature of the germ itself . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Bacteria are complete , free-living cellular organisms , equipped with the biology to survive and reproduce independent of the host they invade . As such , they offer up a variety of relatively easy targets for drug weaponry , though inappropriate use of antibiotics has led to a dire resistance problem . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Viruses essentially consist of nucleic acid &amp;mdash ; genetic material &amp;mdash ; encased in protein . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s really a chemical . It&amp;rsquo ; s inert , &amp;rdquo ; said Duncan . &amp;ldquo ; They&amp;rsquo ; re not alive or dead , they&amp;rsquo ; re inactive or active . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Only when they infiltrate a host , like humans , and glom onto a cell do viruses become active , burrowing into the cell and using its molecular machinery to replicate , creating more viruses to infect other cells . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That&amp;rsquo ; s a difficult , more limited target , and drug developers have to ensure their medicine does not prove toxic to the host cell , as well as the virus attached to it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They&amp;rsquo ; re parasites , by definition that&amp;rsquo ; s what they do &amp;mdash ; they take advantage of a host , &amp;rdquo ; said Duncan . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;6166;TOOLONG ... title= " Scientists are seen working at Cobra Biologics , they are working on a potential vaccine for COVID-19 , following the outbreak of the coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) , in Keele , Britain , April 30 , 2020. " alt= " Scientists are seen working at Cobra Biologics , they are working on a potential vaccine for COVID-19 , following the outbreak of the coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) , in Keele , Britain , April 30 , 2020. " data-portal-copyright= " REUTERS/Carl Recine " **28;6201;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78516605 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; There is another difference that makes viruses harder to target with drugs than bacteria : their tendency to mutate more often and quickly , rendering any medicine targeting them less potent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As Murthy says , &amp;ldquo ; viruses are tricky , they change . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And when people become seriously ill from viral infections , it is often the response of the body&amp;rsquo ; s immune system that does the most damage , creating dangerous inflammation or sepsis. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are immune-suppressing and anti-inflammatory drugs that can treat those symptoms , but anti-virals may be of limited use by that point , even though it&amp;rsquo ; s when many people seek medical help . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Drug development has also been held back by a more practical issue , though also related to how the virus affects humans , says &lt;a href= " https : **33;6231;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;Joanne Lemieux&lt;/a&gt; , a University of Alberta biochemistry professor . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Because flus and other acute viruses can often cause relatively short bouts of disease from which most people recover , the demand for drugs can be limited , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;6266;TOOLONG ... title= " Scientists are seen working at Cobra Biologics , they are working on a potential vaccine for COVID-19 , following the outbreak of the coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) , in Keele , Britain , April 30 , 2020. " alt= " Scientists are seen working at Cobra Biologics , they are working on a potential vaccine for COVID-19 , following the outbreak of the coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) , in Keele , Britain , April 30 , 2020. " data-portal-copyright= " REUTERS/Carl Recine " **28;6301;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78516610 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; And some acute viruses simply disappear . That includes SARS , a coronavirus similar to the one that causes COVID-19 . While in training , Lemieux worked in a lab developing a promising anti-SARS agent , but it never progressed to animal or human trials . Investment in that expensive part of the research dried up as SARS ceased to be an imminent threat , stopped in part by good anti-epidemic campaigns . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In the face of this pandemic , where public health measures are not sufficient , it is going to propel research further , &amp;rdquo ; she predicted . &amp;ldquo ; ( It ) is enhancing and will likely increase the number anti-virals being developed and tested . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Now with her own lab , Lemieux has changed gears like so many other scientists to address SARS-CoV-2 , the new coronavirus . She and colleagues have found that a drug shown to be effective against a fatal , closely related coronavirus in cats is &amp;ldquo ; very promising&amp;rdquo ; in stopping the COVID-19 bug&amp;rsquo ; s replication . And in felines , at least , it has already been proven safe . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Now she is hoping it can move on to human trials . And , if eventually approved , have a possible role in combating the pandemic. &lt;/p&gt; Tom BlackwellSat , 02 May 2020 14:00:43 **45;6331;TOOLONG ... and **33;6378;TOOLONG experts do n't see Remdesivir as a COVID-19 killer : ' This is not a silver bullet'http : **33;6413;TOOLONG ... src= " https : **33;6448;TOOLONG ... title= " An ampule of Ebola drug Remdesivir is pictured during a news conference at the University Hospital Eppendorf ( UKE ) in Hamburg , Germany , April 8 , 2020 , as the spread of coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) continues . &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; The hopeful buzz around the drug Remdesivir and its potential as a COVID-19 treatment grew so deafening Wednesday , it triggered an unexpected surge in the financial markets . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; New evidence &amp;ldquo ; has proven &amp;hellip ; that a drug can block this virus , &amp;rdquo ; enthused Dr. Anthony Fauci , the U.S. government&amp;rsquo ; s top infectious disease expert . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Canadian experts were not quite as exuberant Thursday , saying they need to see more data from a promising U.S. trial before recommending the drug as a standard treatment for the coronavirus. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And even then , its benefits may be limited , they argued . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This is not a silver bullet , this is not going to kill the werewolf , &amp;rdquo ; said Dr. Gerald Evans , chair of the infectious diseases division at Queen&amp;rsquo ; s University . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some also worry about a more fundamental problem : whether there would even be enough of the drug to routinely hand it out here . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One of the Canadian trials studying the unapproved medicine has been unable to obtain sufficient stocks from Gilead Sciences , Remdesivir&amp;rsquo ; s American-based manufacturer , to supply all the trial sites . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If it is proven effective , the challenge with it , and Gilead has told me this many times , is they do not have enough drug for dissemination , &amp;rdquo ; It allows emergency use of unapproved medicine in certain circumstances . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The drug proved ineffective in treating its original target , Ebola virus , but lab and animal studies have suggested it could be useful with patients made sick by the new SARS-CoV-2. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The excitement this week revolved around results from a Remdesivir trial where patients were randomly selected to receive either the drug or a placebo , the gold standard in medical research . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a 1,063-patient trial run by the U.S. government&amp;rsquo ; s National Institutes of Health , those administered Remdesivir recovered in a median of 11 days , 31 per cent faster than the 15 days for those given a placebo . There was also a slight improvement in death rate , but it was not considered statistically significant . The report came with no detailed scientific data , and has not been peer-reviewed. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But the results had to be released quickly because there&amp;rsquo ; s &amp;ldquo ; clear-cut evidence it works&amp;rdquo ; and ethically the medicine should now be offered to patients in the trial&amp;rsquo ; s placebo arm , said Fauci , head of the NIH branch that oversaw the trial . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;6578;TOOLONG ... title= " Lab technicians load filled vials of investigational coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) treatment drug remdesivir at a Gilead Sciences facility in La Verne , California , U.S. March 18 , 2020. The authors did say , though , that more study of the drug is warranted . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The medication has been offered to Canadian patients as part of at least two studies . The children live full-time in their home , while the separated parents take turns staying with them . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But on March 31 , an Ontario &lt;a href= " https : **33;7192;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;judge ordered&lt;/a&gt; that an immune-compromised Ottawa mother be given exclusive use of the matrimonial abode , citing her heightened risk from COVID-19 and the father&amp;rsquo ; s alleged cavalier approach to social distancing . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The parents had actually been sharing the house since the lockdown started . But the eight other customers who later tested positive were not sitting close enough for regular droplet transmission , and most of the patrons and staff avoided infection altogether . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A team of local scientists eventually came to an eye-opening conclusion about the episode : virus-containing particles had hitched a ride on currents created by the eatery&amp;rsquo ; s air-conditioning. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The prevailing view is that the novel coronavirus is transmitted only by close contact with relatively heavy droplets . But for a group of mechanical engineers at the University of Alberta , the finding was no surprise . In their world , they say , it&amp;rsquo ; s well known that building ventilation systems are efficient disseminators of viruses and other pathogens , and they believe the COVID-19 bug is no exception . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Aided by a $440,000 &lt;a href= " https : **33;7682;TOOLONG ... grant&lt;/a&gt; , they&amp;rsquo ; re now working on ways that buildings could change their HVAC set-ups to curb the risk of infection , what the researchers call a&amp;nbsp ; &amp;ldquo ; non-pharmaceutical&amp; ... intervention against the disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We want to save lives , let&amp;rsquo ; s cut right to the chase , &amp;rdquo ; said Prof. Brian Fleck , part of the project . &amp;ldquo ; There are so many , many , many buildings &amp;hellip ; This affects absolutely everybody . Billions of people . If we are able to cut down the transmission rate by a per cent , that&amp;rsquo ; s a lot of people . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The engineers&amp;rsquo ; belief in the importance of building ventilation as a transmitter of the COVID-19 virus is not universally held . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The World Health Organization and &lt;a href= " http : **34;7717;TOOLONG ... public-health bodies&lt;/a&gt; , citing the science to date , say the pathogen is spread almost entirely by droplets , heavier particles emitted mostly when infected people cough or sneeze , and which fall down within a short distance . Hence the two-metre rule for social distancing , and the emphasis on washing hands after touching surfaces where virus may have alighted . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The HVAC systems in most non-medical buildings play only a small role in infectious disease transmission , including COVID-19 , &amp;rdquo ; a committee chair with the &lt;a href= " https : **33;7753;TOOLONG ... Society&lt;/a&gt; of Heating , Refrigerating , and Air-Conditioning Engineers argued last month . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It&amp;rsquo ; s just smaller and lighter aerosol particles that can spread through a ventilation system and &amp;ldquo ; the truth is that we still don&amp;rsquo ; t really know if the ( COVID-19 ) virus can be spread by aerosols , &amp;rdquo ; said &lt;a href= " https : **30;7788;TOOLONG " &g; ... Miller&lt;/a&gt; , a virus expert at McMaster University in Hamilton . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Chinese and Australian air-quality experts , citing in part the experience with SARS , another coronavirus , &lt;a href= " https : **33;7820;TOOLONG ... in a paper&lt;/a&gt; earlier this month that as droplets from an infected person start to evaporate , the resulting smaller particles can indeed become airborne . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; They point to evidence that passengers confined to their cabins on cruise ships like the Diamond Princess may have been infected through the vessels&amp;rsquo ; air ducts . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It is highly likely that the SARS-CoV-2 virus also spreads by air , &amp;rdquo ; they conclude , urging &amp;ldquo ; all possible&amp;rdquo ; action in response , including modifications to ventilation systems . &amp;ldquo ; We predict that &amp;hellip ; failure to immediately recognize and acknowledge the importance of airborne transmission and to take adequate actions against it will result in additional cases . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The HVAC engineers&amp;rsquo ; society itself issued an &lt;a href= " https : **33;7855;TOOLONG ... statement&lt;/a&gt; after the earlier article saying airborne transmission of the new virus was &amp;ldquo ; sufficiently likely&amp;rdquo ; that changes should be made to building ventilation to limit exposure to it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Then there was the Guangzhou restaurant case , detailed in a U.S. Centers for Disease Control online journal recently . &lt;a href= " https : **33;7890;TOOLONG ... concluded&lt;/a&gt; flow from an air conditioner moved over three tables , carrying virus from the infected patron at the middle one to the far table , then back to the diners closest to the air conditioner . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Even if it turned out SARS-CoV-2 does not spread that way , influenza viruses can , and the University of Alberta research would be valuable for that reason alone , said Miller . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Heating , ventilation and air conditioning ( HVAC ) engineers have long known that tiny particles of pathogen travel in the air that is circulated , heated and cooled in modern buildings , said Fleck . He pointed to Legionnaires disease , a bacterial pneumonia first traced to a hotel&amp;rsquo ; s air-conditioning system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This has been on people&amp;rsquo ; s radar for quite a while , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; Somebody on a different floor sneezes &amp;hellip ; The particle can stay airborne long enough to go all the way through the system and then pop out in somebody else&amp;rsquo ; s office . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are various ways that the risk can be lessened , including use of filters that catch a greater number of those particles , and drawing more fresh air into a system . It also is likely that higher levels of humidity &amp;ndash ; a factor that only some Canadian buildings can adjust &amp;ndash ; will help kill off the virus , said Fleck . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But each of those changes carries a cost . Adding more fresh air can require additional heat or air conditioning . Heavier filters means more energy is needed to push the air through them . And more humidity can lead to mould , he noted . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This will make for difficult decision making . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Funded by the Canadian Institutes of Health Research , the University of Alberta project is led by engineering professor Lexuan Zhong and also involves pediatrics professor Lisa Hartling . It consists of three phases : systematically reviewing literature on air circulation and viruses , determining what strategies would be effective and then carrying out a detailed audit of all the buildings on the Edmonton campus to create a real-world model of what should be done . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The team hopes to have solid results by the summer of 2021 , said Fleck . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;em&gt; ( Modified 12:40 April 26 to add comments by Matthew Miller ; modified 4:55 April 28 to correct reference to researchers ' discipline to mechanical engineers ; modified April 29 to add new statement from ASHRAE. ) &lt;/em&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Tom BlackwellSun , 26 Apr 2020 20:30:16 **45;7925;TOOLONG ... and **37;7972;TOOLONG vitamin C help cure COVID-19 ? Since then , the study has enrolled more than 25 pandemic patients with sepsis , says Dr. Neill Adhikari of Toronto&amp;rsquo ; s Sunnbyrook Health Sciences Centre , one of the study&amp;rsquo ; s lead investigators . He hopes to increase that number to as many as 800 , possibly by linking up with a different , international coronavirus trial . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;8115;TOOLONG ... Half will get ascorbic acid infusions , half a placebo. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Most of the patients made critically ill by the new bug suffer from acute respiratory distress syndrome , where the lungs are unable to deliver sufficient oxygen to the blood stream . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But sepsis also occurs &amp;ldquo ; reasonably often&amp;rdquo ; in COVID-19 sufferers , said Adhikari , and can be just as deadly , killing an estimated eight million people worldwide every year . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The condition is difficult to treat , making relatively inexpensive Vitamin C a potentially attractive option , especially in poorer countries , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It has potential . If it&amp;rsquo ; s good , it&amp;rsquo ; s potentially applicable globally , &amp;rdquo ; said Adhikari. &amp;ldquo ; If it&amp;rsquo ; s useful , the implications for uptake are large . If it&amp;rsquo ; s useless , it would be good to know quickly . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , doctors in New York dealing with one of the world&amp;rsquo ; s worst outbreaks of COVID-19 have already been using vitamin C as a treatment , outside of any study , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But not surprisingly , with previous research offering conflicting results , it&amp;rsquo ; s a controversial topic . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A February &lt;a href= " https : **33;8284;TOOLONG ... the influential &lt;em&gt;Journal of the American Medical Association&lt;/em&gt; suggested most of the evidence to date has been negative , and argued against any further trials , or routine use on patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;8319;TOOLONG ... title= " A nurse waits to enter the room of a patient recovering from the COVID-19 virus in the COVID-19 in-patient ward at the Acibadem Altunizade Hospital on April 17 , 2020 in Istanbul , Turkey . " alt= " A nurse waits to enter the room of a patient recovering from the COVID-19 virus in the COVID-19 in-patient ward at the Acibadem Altunizade Hospital on April 17 , 2020 in Istanbul , Turkey . " data-portal-copyright= " Chris McGrath/Getty Images " **28;8354;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78500739 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There appears to be no immediate justification for adoption of high-dose vitamin C , alone or in combination , as a component of treatment for sepsis , &amp;rdquo ; wrote Dr. Andre Kalil of the University of Nebraska Medical Center . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Offering the infusions &amp;ldquo ; just in case&amp;rdquo ; or as a &amp;ldquo ; measure of last resort&amp;rdquo ; could divert needed funding from more promising research , delay proven treatments like antibiotics , and perpetuate &amp;ldquo ; false hopes for patients , families and clinicians , &amp;rdquo ; he said in the commentary . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Vitamin C has a long and contentious history as potential miracle cure , though its promise has gone largely unfulfilled . The discovery that ascorbic acid was medically important is traced to James Lind , a Royal Navy doctor who conducted one of the world&amp;rsquo ; s first controlled trials in 1747 , and found that vitamin C-rich citrus fruits cured sailors afflicted by scurvy . The vitamin itself was not isolated until the early 1900s. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Propelled by advocacy from Linus Pauling , a Nobel laureate in chemistry , the nutrient was examined in the 1960s and 1970s as a possible treatment for the common cold . A &lt;a href= " https : **33;8384;TOOLONG ... review&lt;/a&gt; of previous research concluded it did nothing to prevent or treat colds , but that long-term use might help reduce the duration of them . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;8419;TOOLONG ... title= " " alt= " " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;8454;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;nbsp ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; More recently , alternative-health practitioners have touted vitamin-C infusions as a treatment for cancer , though science has found &lt;a href= " https : **33;8484;TOOLONG ... backing&lt;/a&gt; for their claims . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For sepsis , the theory is that it acts as a modulator of the body&amp;rsquo ; s immune response , an idea that stemmed partly from the fact that vitamin-C levels drop when people develop sepsis. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Interest was piqued by a &lt;a href= " https : **33;8519;TOOLONG ... study&lt;/a&gt; in Virginia that concluded infusions of ascorbic acid significantly curbed the death rate in sepsis patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Among the more rigorous controlled trials since then , one this year by &lt;a href= " https : **33;8554;TOOLONG ... researchers&lt;/a&gt; found no benefit compared to placebo when used on people with septic shock , an advanced form of the condition . Another , &lt;a href= " https : **33;8589;TOOLONG ... last year&lt;/a&gt; , concluded that it reduced the death rate and length of time sepsis patients spent in the ICU . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Salim Surani , a medical professor at Texas A &amp;amp ; M University , still sees promise and urged a &amp;ldquo ; relentless pursuit&amp;rdquo ; of research in a &lt;a href= " https : **33;8624;TOOLONG ... commentary&lt;/a&gt; last year . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He even suggested providing vitamin C to mild COVID-19 patients , before they develop sepsis. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; You have a medicine that is very , very cheap ( and ) has kind of a proven track record in a substantial number of patients , &amp;rdquo ; said Surani in an interview . &amp;ldquo ; If something is working and &amp;hellip ; doesn&amp;rsquo ; t have harmful side effects , we have pretty much nothing to lose and something to gain . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As long as enough patients can be enrolled before the pandemic ends , Toronto&amp;rsquo ; s Adhikari expects results from his trial as soon as a year from now . &lt;/p&gt; Tom BlackwellMon , 20 Apr 2020 16:06:26 **45;8659;TOOLONG ... **41;8706;TOOLONG HealthcareTorontoWorld " That is a surprise " : Doctors still waiting for feared surge of COVID-19 patients in Canadian ICUshttp : **36;8749;TOOLONG ... src= " https : **33;8787;TOOLONG ... title= " A directions sign is seen at Sunnybrook Hospital in Toronto , Ontario , Canada January 26 , 2020. " alt= " A directions sign is seen at Sunnybrook Hospital in Toronto , Ontario , Canada January 26 , 2020. " data-portal-copyright= " REUTERS/Carlos Osorio " **28;8822;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78490865 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; They have been bracing for the worst , a deluge of desperately sick COVID-19 patients who overwhelm resources and lead to chilling decisions over who gets life-saving care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Doctors , nurses and other staff who run the nation&amp;rsquo ; s intensive-care units have feared for their own safety , too , amid shortages of protective equipment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But , perhaps surprisingly , some critical-care physicians in the hardest-hit provinces say they have yet to face that feared surge of coronavirus sufferers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; So far , at least , there is no flood , and plenty of available ICU beds. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Ontario , &amp;nbsp ; almost 78 per cent of the province&amp;rsquo ; s expanded ventilator capacity remained free.The patient volume was well under the &amp;ldquo ; best case&amp;rdquo ; scenario depicted in Ontario-government epidemic modelling released earlier this month . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Quebec has seen a modest eight to 10 COVID-19 admissions a day to the ICU recently , and none at all from April 7 to April 8 , said Dr. Fr&amp;eacute ; d&amp;eacute ; rick D&amp;rsquo ; aragon of the University of Sherbrooke , his province&amp;rsquo ; s spokesman for the Canadian Critical Care Society . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In my centre , we have still a lot of empty beds in the ICU , &amp;rdquo ; said D&amp;rsquo ; Aragon late last week . &amp;ldquo ; That is a surprise . We changed all our schedules , our ICU schedules three weeks ago , to be on call . We cancelled all the teaching classes , all other administrative activities &amp;hellip ; So far , today , I&amp;rsquo ; m at home . It&amp;rsquo ; s not what we expected . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At Toronto&amp;rsquo ; s Sunnybrook Health Sciences Centre , which treated Canada&amp;rsquo ; s first-known COVID-19 patient , no one is taking anything for granted , but the intensive-care unit is definitely not over-run , said Dr. Gordon Rubenfeld , a critical-care physician there . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Is Sunnybrook right now being stressed by the number of COVID patients ? &amp;hellip ; &amp;ldquo ; I would say &amp;lsquo ; No , &amp;rsquo ; &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; I&amp;rsquo ; m cautiously optimistic we&amp;rsquo ; re not going to see the giant surge and nightmare that we&amp;rsquo ; ve seen in Italy and New York . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Critical-care doctors are quick to add caveats &amp;ndash ; that the worst may still be to come , and that even the slow daily accumulation of COVID-19 patients &amp;ndash ; who often spend weeks on a ventilator &amp;ndash ; could gradually fill up ICUs and weigh down the system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In a month , we may be suffering from not the giant surge that they saw in New York , but basically a very slow filling of the pool , &amp;rdquo ; said Rubenfeld &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And the fact every new critical-care patient is treated as a PUI &amp;ndash ; person under investigation for COVID-19 &amp;ndash ; means staff must constantly don protective equipment , a stressful and time-consuming process , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Physicians also note it is crucial for people to abide by stay-at-home directives , which they suspect have suppressed the number of patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But even so , the dearth of apocalyptic scenes in Canada&amp;rsquo ; s ICUs seems to offer at least a glimmer of hope , especially after the release of federal government modeling last week that predicted 11,000 COVID-19 deaths in a best-case scenario . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And while statistics on the total number of people testing positive for the disease vary with the amount of testing a province does , data on seriously ill people admitted to hospital and ICU would seem to offer a more reliable barometer of the epidemic&amp;rsquo ; s severity and path . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I&amp;rsquo ; m cautiously optimistic that we are going to see a sort of slow trickle with this concept of flattening the curve , &amp;rdquo ; said Dr. Michael Detsky of Toronto&amp;rsquo ; s Mount Sinai Hospital . &amp;ldquo ; We&amp;rsquo ; ll still see patients getting admitted to our ICU , but not that surge like we saw first in Wuhan , then in Italy , Iran , Spain , New York City &amp;ndash ; and now some other U.S. cities . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Doctors say they&amp;rsquo ; re unsure exactly why they have not seen more cases , but point to both the mass , at-home social-distancing measures still in place , and a cancellation of thousands of elective surgeries that freed up space . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A detailed report on ICU admissions in Ontario obtained by the National Post hints at a positive trend . It includes both confirmed and suspected coronavirus cases and shows a peak on April 2 of 538 possible COVID-19 sufferers in critical care . The number has remained at between about 500 and 535 since then &amp;ndash ; out of a total number of ICU patients that stood at 1,321 last Thursday . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The &amp;ldquo ; best case&amp;rdquo ; scenario in Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s modelling had put the number of COVID-19 sufferers in intensive care at between 800 and 1,000 by last Friday . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And almost 78 per cent of the province&amp;rsquo ; s ventilator capacity &amp;ndash ; expanded to try to meet the pandemic demand &amp;ndash ; was still available , according to the Critical Care Services Ontario document . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Quebec was reporting just under 200 COVID-19 patients in the ICU as of Friday , out of an expanded capacity of 1,000 available critical care beds , said D&amp;rsquo ; Aragon. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That said , the total number of patients admitted to hospitals does seem to be rising faster in Quebec than the figures for ICUs specifically , he noted . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It has in Ontario , too . An average of about 50 laboratory-confirmed COVID-19 patients a day have been admitted to hospital in the last week and a half , according to publicly available government figures . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And it&amp;rsquo ; s possible the number of people dying from COVID-19 in hospital is outstripping the new admissions , keeping the occupancy rate down &amp;ndash ; and making the situation look brighter than it actually is , said Dr. Michael Warner , ICU director at Toronto&amp;rsquo ; s Michael Garron Hospital . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; After Quebec and Ontario , British Columbia and Alberta have the largest number of confirmed coronavirus cases . One Alberta physician suggested ICUs there have yet to face a surge . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We have been very fortunate in Calgary to have not been strained , &amp;rdquo ; Dr. Tom Stelfox , head of critical-care medicine at the University of Calgary , said by email . &amp;ldquo ; The numbers of patients we have cared for in ICUs in Calgary have been manageable and provided us with important experience in caring for these patients . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;em&gt; ( Modified 8 a.m . " data-portal-copyright= " THE CANADIAN PRESS/Ryan Remiorz " **28;9298;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78477725 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Thomas Hemmerling , a University of Montreal anesthesiology professor , said he and other physicians have been trying to get the word to government for two weeks that it should use Hotel Dieu . He has little doubt it will be needed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; At the end of the day , it doesn&amp;rsquo ; t matter how you look at it &amp;hellip ; there will be , very early in the epidemic , a lack of space and ICU beds , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;strong&gt; " &lt;/strong&gt; There are many countries building makeshift hospitals ; we have an existing one right smack in the middle of the city . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Other nations hard hit by the pandemic have also opened temporary hospitals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; China famously built two COVID-19 centres in Wuhan from scratch in a matter of days . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In the U.S. , the Federal Emergency Management Agency is building four , 250-bed hospitals at a New York City convention centre , while the U.S. army is setting up two field hospitals in Long Island , N.Y . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ontario health ministry has asked health-care facilities to try to find extra space that could be used for patient care . Runnymede Healthcare Centre , a rehab hospital in west-end Toronto , is already taking up the call , first by converting a non-patient space into a 40-bed temporary ward , said CEO Connie Dejak. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The project &amp;mdash ; designed to take non-COVID patients if acute-care hospitals become overburdened by coronavirus sufferers &amp;mdash ; is being guided by volunteer help from builder Carmine Nigro of Craft Developments , who is also chairman of the Liquor Control Board of Ontario . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It should give people comfort that you have such a co-ordinated system behind the scenes planning for whatever the need may be , &amp;rdquo ; said Dejak. &amp;ldquo ; And they&amp;rsquo ; re proactive . That&amp;rsquo ; s what we need to be . We need to be ready . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; B.C. Health Minister Adrian Dix announced Tuesday the landmark convention centre in Vancouver &amp;mdash ; unused now because of the ban on large gatherings &amp;mdash ; would be converted into a medical centre . The province is also expected to repurpose community centres and other public spaces elsewhere , if more overflow space is needed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Joseph Brant Hospital in Burlington is building a modular structure outside its main complex to house 93 COVID-19 patients , if necessary , the facility announced Monday . &lt;/p&gt; Tom BlackwellWed , 01 Apr 2020 17:05:09 **45;9328;TOOLONG ... But it also contained some startling information . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Haliburton , Kawartha , Pine Ridge District Health Unit had &lt;a href= " https : **33;9504;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;just announced&lt;/a&gt; that three people at the Pinecrest Nursing Home in Bobcaygeon , Ont. , had tested positive for COVID-19 . What the public-health officials didn&amp;rsquo ; t mention , said Jones in a &lt;a href= " https : **33;9539;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;note disseminated&lt;/a&gt; over social media last weekend , was that 20 other residents and eight staff members were suffering from similar symptoms , but had yet to be tested . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This additional 28 people undoubtedly are further cases of COVID-19 , &amp;rdquo ; Jones wrote . &amp;ldquo ; This means that there could be hundreds of cases in the community that have gone undetected . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;9574;TOOLONG ... Taiwan and Singapore managed to contain COVID-19 , while letting normal life go on&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;9609;TOOLONG ... Neither Jones , the health unit nor Pinecrest administrators could be reached for comment . But there&amp;rsquo ; s little evidence government officials have issued any warnings about the risk of community spread from the facility , or tried to contact those who might be affected . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Orders to stay at home and practice social distancing on a mass scale are in effect across the country . It&amp;rsquo ; s unclear , however , to what extent public-health authorities are still doing the traditional forms of epidemic detective work : strictly quarantining those infected and tracking down people who might have encountered them while they were contagious &amp;mdash ; what&amp;rsquo ; s called contact tracing . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Such methods have been pursued aggressively by countries like &lt;a href= " https : **33;9741;TOOLONG ... target= " blank " rel= " noopener " &gt;Taiwan , Singapore&lt;/a&gt; and South Korea , allowing them to get the virus somewhat under control , while avoiding large-scale lockdowns like Canada&amp;rsquo ; s. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Asking everyone to stay home is important but only a &amp;ldquo ; defensive measure , &amp;rdquo ; Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus , head of the World Health Organization , &lt;a href= " https : **33;9776;TOOLONG ... Monday&lt;/a&gt; . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; To win , we need to attack the virus with aggressive and targeted tactics &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; testing every suspected case , isolating and caring for every confirmed case , and tracing and quarantining every close contact . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canadian officials insist that&amp;rsquo ; s what&amp;rsquo ; s being done here . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Case identification and very rigorous contact tracing is still being carried out in every province and local municipality , &amp;rdquo ; Dr. Theresa Tam , Canada&amp;rsquo ; s chief public health officer , said Monday . &amp;ldquo ; That is still Canada&amp;rsquo ; s strategy &amp;hellip ; We&amp;rsquo ; re still containing the virus as much as possible . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;9811;TOOLONG ... title= " Canada 's Chief Public Health Officer Dr. Theresa Tam speaks at a news conference on the coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) outbreak on Parliament Hill in Ottawa , Ontario , Canada March 19 , 2020. " alt= " Canada 's Chief Public Health Officer Dr. Theresa Tam speaks at a news conference on the coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) outbreak on Parliament Hill in Ottawa , Ontario , Canada March 19 , 2020. " data-portal-copyright= " REUTERS/Blair Gable " **28;9846;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78466643 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; But with an estimated one million &lt;a href= " https : **33;9876;TOOLONG ... returning to the country from overseas&lt;/a&gt; in recent days , there&amp;rsquo ; s evidence public-health officials are struggling to keep up . The case of one P.E.I. woman who had symptoms of what turned out to be COVID-19 , stayed overnight in Toronto and then flew to Moncton last week has received little attention . Close to 80 Canadian passengers from a cruise ship rife with the virus say they faced minimal screening when they returned on commercial flights this past weekend . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Jeff Kwong , a family physician and public-health professor at the University of Toronto , says continued contact tracing is &amp;ldquo ; absolutely&amp;rdquo ; necessary to identify where outbreaks are occurring and halt the chain of transmission . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He admits he has no inside knowledge of what&amp;rsquo ; s happening in the local public-health offices that run the ground war against any epidemic , but senses it is becoming increasingly difficult for them to stay on top of the situation . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Part of the problem , Kwong said , is chronic under-funding. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But COVID-19 poses unique challenges . Patients tend to first develop mild symptoms they may not pay heed to , and there&amp;rsquo ; s some evidence even asymptomatic people can be contagious . Then there can also be a delay in getting a test , and test results . So once someone is confirmed as COVID-19 positive , public-health tracers have to look back several days for possible contacts , said Kwong . Meanwhile , those contacts may already have the disease &amp;mdash ; which has a relatively short incubation period of five days &amp;mdash ; and be spreading it themselves . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This might be contributing to why they are so over-stretched , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; You&amp;rsquo ; re just constantly trying to play catch-up. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When the novel coronavirus first reached Canada in late January , the public-health response seemed more stringent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Authorities said at the time they were contacting passengers on a flight that this country&amp;rsquo ; s first recorded case took from China to Toronto . Canadians repatriated from Wuhan &amp;mdash ; where the pandemic started &amp;mdash ; and later from contaminated cruise ships , were held in a quarantine facility at Canadian Forces Base Trenton. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But things were dramatically different for Martha Turner Bradbury and her husband Al this past weekend when the Winnipegers came back from Marseilles , France after being let off the Costa Luminosa . Several passengers on the cruise ship have &lt;a href= " https : **33;9911;TOOLONG ... positive&lt;/a&gt; for COVID-19 . About 75 Canadians and 230 Americans were flown to Atlanta , Ga. , on a crowded plane , where Bradbury estimates one in 10 passengers had visible respiratory symptoms &amp;ndash ; fever , coughs , looking generally unwell . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **33;9946;TOOLONG ... title= " Passengers aboard the Costa Luminosa cruise ship , that was hit by the coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) , wave from a balcony , at the port of Savona , near Genoa , in Italy , March 21 , 2020. " alt= " Passengers aboard the Costa Luminosa cruise ship , that was hit by the coronavirus disease ( COVID-19 ) , wave from a balcony , at the port of Savona , near Genoa , in Italy , March 21 , 2020. " data-portal-copyright= " REUTERS/Massimo Pinca " **28;9981;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 78466654 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; But anyone who didn&amp;rsquo ; t have symptoms was allowed to move freely into the Atlanta terminal and then board commercial flights . The Bradburys took one to Minneapolis , and another from there to Winnipeg . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A Canadian Borders Services Agency officer had them don masks and gloves when they disclosed their recent contact with COVID-19 patients , asked about their arrangements for self-isolation , then let them go . Bradbury says a couple they travelled with were not even questioned about possible virus contact . Now in isolation , Winnipeg public health officials have told them they won&amp;rsquo ; t be tested unless they develop symptoms . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Honestly , we expected there to be far more strict guidelines when you admit you were heavily exposed in a pretty confined area , &amp;rdquo ; said Bradbury. &amp;ldquo ; To go from complete isolation in your cabin on the ship to all of a sudden to just be out in the general population &amp;hellip ; There needs to be some kind of tracking system . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; P.E.I. reported last week that a woman who had been on another cruise ship flew to Toronto and stayed at a hotel there before flying on to Moncton March 9 , on WestJet Flight 3440 . New Brunswick&amp;rsquo ; s chief medical officer of health &lt;a href= " https : **33;10011;TOOLONG ... anyone&lt;/a&gt; on the flight to stay in isolation and monitor their symptoms . But there is no indication public health proactively contacted the passengers . Nor has there been any official notice to Toronto residents who may have encountered the woman . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Toronto public health will follow up on contacts with an infected person if notified of any by counterparts in another province , spokeswoman Dr. Lisa Berger said by email . Her office did not respond when asked if it had received such notification . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Toronto was the first and is one of the hardest-hit cities in Canada . Contact tracing is still an important part of the public health department&amp;rsquo ; s response to COVID-19 , Berger said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But while places like Singapore and Taiwan have recruited police officers and are using cell-phone technology and even &lt;a href= " https : **27;10046;TOOLONG ? fbcli ... cell-phone apps&lt;/a&gt; to trace contacts , the effort in Canada seems more constrained . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Toronto&amp;rsquo ; s department prioritizes notifying close household contacts and those in health-care settings and places with vulnerable people , &amp;ldquo ; followed by other close contacts and all other contacts , &amp;rdquo ; said Berger . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And while Singapore , for instance , sends text messages several times a day and makes random phone calls and visits to check up on quarantined patients , Toronto acts only if it becomes aware someone with COVID-19 is not isolating . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Our staff would counsel the person , try to understand the barriers to isolation and mitigate the barriers , &amp;rdquo ; Berger said . If that didn&amp;rsquo ; t work , it would resort to legal means , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Kwong said he has no doubt public-health officials Canada-wide are &amp;ldquo ; pulling max overtime&amp;rdquo ; to fight the pandemic . But with a million Canadians recently arriving from other countries and possibly importing the virus , the earlier , tough measures &amp;mdash ; like securing people at the Trenton base &amp;mdash ; may simply not be possible . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s one thing if it&amp;rsquo ; s a flight or two , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; How can you put a million people into bases ? &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; Tom BlackwellTue , 24 Mar 2020 19:05:32 **45;10075;TOOLONG ... **45;10122;TOOLONG ... let us down ' : Emergency MDs say they 're getting garbled government messages about COVID-19http : **32;10169;TOOLONG ... src= " https : **33;10203;TOOLONG ... title= " A health care worker speaks to a test patient , a staff member portraying the role of a patient to ensure the assessment systems are working , at an assessment table as they prepare for the opening of the COVID-19 Assessment Centre at Brewer Park Arena in Ottawa , during a media tour on Friday , March 13 , 2020.
